ParlaMint-SI_2021-03-03-SDZ8-Izredna-63.ana.u259	Thank you very much. Luka Mesec will present the position of the Left Group. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2021-03-03-SDZ8-Izredna-63.ana.u260	Thank you very much and very much! I'll start with a little broader introduction. In 1990, in Slovenia, 30% of all apartments were rented. Then, by privatisation following a rapid procedure, with the so-called Jazbinšk Law, the share of public rentals in Slovenia has decreased to 3% in only 10 years. Slovenia is currently a European country, I think we are even in the first place in the European Union after how few public rentals we have. If you go a little north of us, to Austria or Germany, you will see that there is a percentage of public rentals above what we had in 1990, that is 30%. And that's where the problem came in. If an apartment in Yugoslav socialism was perceived as some kind of right to a roof over the head, the Slovenian Constitution considers it a right after all, privatisation and capitalism have turned it into goods. It is said that, from a social right, the apartment has been turned into a completely marketable commodity in which only the market can be affected and which can only be traded on the market, not on the basis of needs, but on the basis of the ability to pay. And so we slowly got into today's situation where, if you look around you, even after major cities in Slovenia, the prices of apartments fly to the sky. In Ljubljana and along the coast, you will also find some cellars that go for half the minimum wage and more. The most obvious consequence of the privatisation of the public housing fund is the chronic lack of public rental housing, which is a condition that the state can regulate rent prices in any way and beyond. In fact, rent prices and housing prices should be read together. If rents are high, it is also high in housing prices, because, as you probably know, a lot of people buy apartments in Slovenia today as an investment. Two of my acquaintances who sell the apartment say that virtually everyone else who comes says, "I buy it as an investment, not buying this apartment for myself, but buying it to rent it. There are also those who come with foreign capital, you will meet a lot of Russians recently in Ljubljana, who are buying apartments as an investment in this purely true sense, only because of speculation that the prices of real estate in this city or in Slovenia will continue to rise. To buy 200 grand today, three years from now, this could be worth more and it's worth it. And now that productive investments are lacking for people with capital, housing is usually an investment in which capital is withdrawn at such times. Also from Germany, if you read the reports, it has been reported that during the complete economic contraction last year real estate prices practically increased throughout Germany, as capital just withdrew into investments there. The key task that the State of Slovenia should perform under such circumstances is to build at least a minimum fund for public rentals as soon as possible. Research shows that there are approximately 10 000 people missing from public rental apartments in Slovenia, of which 6 000 700 are in urban municipalities, 4 000 200 in the Municipality of Ljubljana. And public rentals haven't been built in years. At the same time, on the other hand, the rich market for commercial rental apartments, where rents fly to the sky and the prices of apartments fly to the sky, the youngest generation who are now entering the labour market and who would like to arrange a roof over their heads. Those with regular jobs are still receiving loans that will be repaid for 20, 25 years. They don't even get credit in their pens. It is absolutely imperative that the state arranges this in a way that begins to build public rental apartments and above that ground the prices of real estate and rental prices. Another consequence of this privatisation in the 1990s is the difficult management and maintenance of multi-housing buildings, which are now heavily fragmented all over Slovenia and therefore poses a major problem in reaching consensus for construction operations, energy renovations and other major maintenance and investment works. And all I have said now are the facts in the light of which we need to judge the novel of the Housing Law that we are considering today. In the opinion of the Left, this also contains both good solutions and some that are unacceptable to us and will be taken up in the following points. In Levica, we first welcome the regulations adopted by law in the area of property law relations, the management of multi-housing buildings and the related inspection controls. We also welcome the increase in the possibility of borrowing public housing funds, which will lead to debt capital that will enable public housing to be built. And this is a solution that we have already proposed in 2017. We welcome the changes in non-profit rent subsidies, in particular the weighting of the minimum income to calculate the subsidy for single-member households, and the raising of the maximum non-profit rent subsidy from 80 % to 85 % of the non-profit rent and the raising of the subsidy to market rent. We also agree with the gradual increase in the value of the point to determine the value of the non-profit rent from 2.63 euros to 3.5 euros and the forecast of annual coordination. This will raise stagnant non-profit rents, but, as it is said, the subsidy increases simultaneously, so that it will not affect low-income households; on the other hand, it will allow the funds to start at least about their own apartments, which they own, to depreciate because the problem is that the non-profit rent has not been harmonised for nearly 15 years. And if you listen to housing fund managers, they will tell you all over Slovenia that not only do they have no investments, their income from non-profit rent is so low that they have no money to depreciate what they currently own. Then there were various bypasses that the housing funds had investment money they had instead of investing in non-profit apartments in the construction of luxury market apartments in order to obtain funds for their basic business by selling them. This is a story, say the Novo Brdo project in Ljubljana. In this way, housing funds have also contributed to the deterioration of already bad trends in the real estate market, so it was essential that this situation be rectified. However, the law also has something for us that is unacceptable. The first, which is unclear, is the shortening of the notice period in the event of a delay in the payment of the rent and the shortening of the time limit for eviction in this case determined by the court. Here you have to say, Slovenia is according to international indicators, you will find them best explained on the Global Property Guide web platform, which countries categorize as how their legislation is adapted to renters or lessors, you will see this picture there. In the European Union, 15 countries have legislation that is favourable to the tenants. These are the countries that have the best-ordered housing policy – Scandinavian countries, Germany, Austria and so on, they are usually countries that have a strong public lease fund. Then there are six countries that have a neutral policy where the power relationship between the lessor and the lessor is approximately levelled; and six countries that have legislation for tenants is extremely unfavourable. And, of course, Slovenia is among the latter, among the countries which already have some kind of unfavourable legislation for tenants. If you want proof or some illustration of life, look around and ask the people you know and rent an apartment, who has a contract for long-term rental in a market apartment. Not in a public rental, but in a market. I've been renting apartments myself for almost ten years, and I've never had a contract that's longer than a year. For one year it is being renewed and the tenant is not in any way protected from rising prices. When the contract ends at the end of the year, you start negotiating for a new one; and if you don't agree to a price hike, the lessor just replaces you with someone else. I know a lot of people who have been in very plentiful situations during the coronary crisis, and their lenders during the crisis, even though these people, the tenants, suffered a loss of income, increased their rent prices. We do not understand why in this case the power ratios, which are already fully in favour of the lenders in Slovenia, would increase. Additional power is given only by the fact that rental apartments are lacking on the market; and because they are rare, prices are rising or can raise prices. Here we should go in the opposite direction, forcing the tenants to rent the apartment for long-term rent or at least medium-term rent, and for renters to have some power within this arrangement to negotiate conditions of stay, price and others; not that their contracts change each year. Another controversial solution to the law is the public rental of apartments. We've got questions here, in particular. The law foresees that the Housing Fund will rent apartments in which it will play the role of an intermediary, manager and tenant manager, who will rent them from private individuals and be able to pay for 30% more than a non-profit rent. The question that arises here is this. Why are these apartments, where the Housing Fund will rent apartments, on the market empty? I think that this solution is supposed to be empty because at the time, with the Law of Jasbinsk, people who are probably already in years and are no longer concerned about what kind of apartment they get and is empty, but who alone have no will or energy to deal with it; and therefore will give it to the Housing Fund for public rent. The Housing Fund will fully relieve them of any obligations with regard to this apartment. I think that's the assumption on which it's written. But as I said at the outset, the problem is that investors enter Slovenia, especially Ljubljana, who do not have empty apartments because they would be in years and powerless to deal with them, but who explicitly buy apartments as an investment. In this respect, we are concerned that this trend will deteriorate further. If someone who can live on the expectation that prices on the market will continue to increase, they would be able to afford as some kind of speculative investment, in fact they would be paid to give this flat simply to the Housing Fund and be burdened by any obligation in this regard. In fact, he gets a state subsidy for this his speculative investment. And this is a question for the Ministry, and I'm wondering how it's going to answer that. If I withdraw, we will, due to some reservations about this law, especially the last two which I have said, abstain in the first reading, and in the second, we will table amendments to try to correct the shortcomings of the law or things that we do not think are right; and we will bind our support in view of the adoption of these amendments.

ParlaMint-SI_2021-03-03-SDZ8-Izredna-63.ana.u262	Thank you for your word. Dear colleagues and colleagues! The proposed Act on Amendments and Supplements to the Housing Law primarily solves the most pressing problems in the area of multi-housing buildings, non-profit rentals and rental apartments. I am afraid, however, that the changes introduced in these areas by the proposal of the law are only a means of putting the fire out. A more comprehensive overhaul of the Housing Law will soon require further discussion. The bill reduces the required consensus in the area of multi-housing buildings, making it easier to carry out various maintenance works and reconstruction works in multi-housing buildings. Following the current regulation, it is virtually impossible to obtain agreement on construction works in common parts of the building for which a building permit has to be obtained, since it is necessary to obtain agreement from all the floor owners, it is said to be 100%, while the new bill reduces the required consent by only more than 75% of the floor owners. Of course, it should be pointed out that the proposal for a law provides for legal protection for the voting of an essential owner who will be able to challenge the decision in an unfair proceeding before a court of law. The possibility of e-voting high-level owners on the management of a multi-family building will certainly help to speed up negotiations and consensus-building. In the area of non-profit rents, the bill increases the value of the point as a basis for determining non-profit rent, from the current value of 2.63 euros to 3.50 euros, with a three-year transitional period with a gradual increase. However, following the implementation of the full height of the dwelling point, the annual alignment of the point with the increase in the cost of living is envisaged. The value of the point has not changed since the adoption of the euro, i.e. since 2007, due to the absence of a legal basis. As a result, the real value of the non-profit rent fell year after year and simply no longer covered the necessary costs of using the apartment. After fourteen years, the situation is so hot that housing funds and municipalities sell non-profit apartments, since they are no longer able to cover the cost of using the apartment, and at the same time there is no interest in the construction or purchase of new rental apartments. This is also the main reason why waiting periods for the granting of a non-profit apartment only extend. The table below shows that on the basis of the higher value of the point, a non-profit rent of 65 square metres would increase from the current 253.50 euros to 337,35 euros. The bill also foresees an increase in non-profit rent subsidies, which means that the amount of rent would not be increased for those weakest families in particular. The main objective of the bill is to address the pressing problem of housing shortages. In Nova Slovenija, we strongly hope that, at the expense of the increase in the point of non-profit rent and the ability to increase the indebtedness of housing funds in the medium and long term in Slovenia, a substantial number of public apartments will be built. In the short term, however, it is necessary to activate existing unoccupied dwellings as quickly as possible. By the way, there is an estimate that there are as many as 25,000 such apartments in Ljubljana. In Nova Slovenija, we hope that by means of a public rental service, unlike new constructions, it will be possible to provide as many new public rental apartments as possible, since the financial implications are significantly lower than the possible investment in the construction of an additional 2 000 public rental apartments, provided by the public rental service since 2025. The Housing Fund of the Republic of Slovenia will be able to pay the owner a maximum of 1,3 times the non-profit rent for comparable housing. In practice, this means that the owner would receive a maximum of €438 via the public rental service by renting an apartment in Ljubljana, 65 square metres, while the owner would be able to deliver it for €550 to €900 on the market. In the case of the law, therefore, it is not an infringement of the free market for dwellings that are already being handed over in one way or another, but rather an incentive to give up those dwellings that would otherwise remain empty because the owners are older or people who do not want to deal with red tape are afraid of problematic tenants and so on. In New Slovenia, we believe that other measures, particularly in the field of tax policy, should be introduced in order to free up other rental apartments, in particular the reduction in the tax rate for renting apartments, as this, thanks to the previous government, represents as much as 27.5%. As a result, many apartments turn out in black or do not give out at all. Moreover, greater protection of owners will also be needed to increase the interest of owners in renting apartments. A number of comments were made by local communities on the proposal. In Novi Slovenije we are convinced that the proposal of the law offers municipalities more resources for the construction of new rental apartments due mainly to higher non-profit rents and the possibility of more indebtedness of housing funds. Let me stress, however, that part of the financial burden on municipalities in the area of housing has also been taken away from the Law on the Deploration of Communities, in which the total cost of subsidisation of market rents from municipalities has been transferred to the state budget, and the municipalities have also received higher averages. We have stated in the New Slovenia programme that every citizen, in particular families with children, must have a realistic opportunity to ensure at least a minimum standard of living, in their own apartment, rented apartment or apartment house. The affordable housing must be part of the promise of the welfare of the social market economy. Since at least part of the proposal goes in this direction, in Nova Slovenija we believe it is suitable as such for further consideration, where we will try to improve it further with our proposals. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2021-03-03-SDZ8-Izredna-63.ana.u264	Honourable Vice-President, Honourable Secretary of State, Fellow Member and Members of Parliament! The existing housing law entered into force in October 2003. The law, which replaced the so-called 1991 Jasbin law, is still in force today and has been amended and amended four times in these years. Today we are discussing the proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Housing Law, which the Government adopted and submitted to the National Assembly in January. The amendment of the law thus again merely partially interferes with certain parts of the housing policy. A key amendment to the law is the harmonisation of the non-profit rent, which will gradually increase by 33% for the first time since 2007. In comparison, a large apartment will have to pay EUR 239 for a new 45 square metres, so far 179. In doing so, the rent subsidy will increase to a maximum of 85 % of the recognised non-profit rent. So far, this limit has been 80%. At the same time, subsidies for renting market apartments will also increase. One of the main reasons for the collapse of the public rental fund is the inadequate amount of non-profit rent, which makes it impossible for the funds to maintain the existing housing fund, let alone invest in the construction of new ones. The existing non-profit rent does not cover the costs of the operation of the Fund, nor does the State provide funds to cover and recover the unpaid liabilities of the tenants for which the subsidiary fund guarantees. It would be appropriate for the State to take over and provide funds if it imposes such obligations. At the Ministry, it is estimated that there are now 20 to 30 000 privately owned dwellings in the country that are not located on the rental market. There is no accurate data, as there is no cadastre of housing use which would allow the state and local communities to monitor developments in the housing rental market and react to anomalies, in this sense to unreasonable real-time prices. Part of this solution will be the establishment of a promised public rental service that will enable the earliest rental of many vacant apartments that are unoccupied. The fund will rent apartments on the market and rent them further. With the role of an intermediary, manager and tenant manager, it will relieve housing owners of all transactions and risks associated with the granting of housing. An additional opportunity is presented by apartments that are in relatively poor condition, but could be activated by moderate investment. Unfortunately, the bill does not address them. Thus, the public rental service could assume the role of an institution that would restore and restore the facility to function, thus taking over some of the functions of public-private partnership as we know it in the case of energy contracting and being carried out in the case of a number of public buildings such as schools, libraries and other public buildings. In the Resolution on the National Housing Programme 2015-2025, it is estimated that almost a quarter of the rental apartments are illegally leased. The reasons differ from high and high taxes to the unpredictability of the rental relationship and mechanisms should be put in place to establish and strengthen trust between lenders and tenants. The SAB priority is housing for young people who, due to precarious forms of employment and unreasonablely high rents and housing prices, have difficulties in self-reliance. A clear signal from the government regarding measures and the achievement of the objectives of the real estate market is therefore essential to make rents and equity more accessible. In the SAB, we take the problem of housing very seriously, and we regret that instead of a comprehensive renovation of housing legislation, the government has only adopted some, otherwise welcome amendments. The Government also agreed, inter alia, to the mandatory separate fiduciary accounts for the reserve fund of each multi-inhabitation building separately, to reduce the number of necessary consents of the key owners for improvements and construction works requiring the building permit and to reduce the cancellation deadlines of the lease. During the changes, they also highlighted the preliminary right of the Housing Fund of the Republic of Slovenia to sell walled municipal land intended for multi-residential construction. As enshrined in the Constitution, the state is creating opportunities for citizens to obtain adequate housing. It follows that it should operate actively and mediate on the real estate market when anomalies occur. However, this is not possible without the possibility to review the situation on the market in real time. As we explain to ourselves Slovenians and Slovenians that in times of crisis, when people lost their income, the security of their jobs has become less finished and many apartments are unoccupied, real estate prices are rising. The enslavement of generations with 30-year loans is not an answer that the state and government could be proud of. Therefore, at the level of the country, the target rent levels should be defined, including indirect housing prices, and measures should be adapted accordingly. In this way, non-profit rents would increase slightly, and in proportion to this, subsidies to beneficiaries, which is essential, would fall to market rents. We will therefore not oppose the proposal in the SAB, but we expect the government to take measures to address the issue in an integrated manner. Thank you very much.

There are different cooperative models, they can be owned, leased or combined. Here we are not talking about a full-time pension, but the absolute lowest pension anyone in this country can receive. 442 Euros are charged the minimum cost of living for a short period of time and it seemed appropriate for us to anchor the lowest amount of pension we could recover. The second measure proposed, the lowest pension, this time for the full 40 years, is put at the level of long-term cost of living, namely EUR 613. No one in this country who spent 40 years building this society, which also built the country itself, deserves to live in poverty. EUR 613 is the most bare minimum that such a person should receive in order to enjoy at least a measure of safe age. And third, what we propose is that such changes have a positive impact and that other transfers that are linked to the pension register are recalculated. In particular, what hurts us particularly and exposes us is compensation in the event of disability and widow's pensions fall in here and so on. All these three changes, which we have made in our short amendment, should also apply to people who are now in retirement and who are already enjoying pension status and not just for future pensioners. This is a few small marriage articles, which would greatly contribute to the eradication of poverty among pensioners, who is the farthest exposed group to the risk of poverty in that country, especially the elderly female in rural areas and so on. It is likely that this amendment will not be voted on, it will not be voted on with some silent argument that there is no money for pensioners. Funds for bank guarantees are found, funds are found to recapitalise firms in difficulty, and when poverty is resolved, these funds are suddenly lacking. For this measure, we would need 160 million euros. In Levica we think we should find these 160 million euros to help people, be careful!, not live in luxury, but survive. As I say, I will vote in favour of raising the lowest pensions in this country.
